AT  the  General  Annual  Meeting  of  the  SURTEES  SOCIETY,  held 

( 

in  the  Warden's  Room,  in  the  Castle  of  Durham,  on  the 
fifteenth  of  December,  1853, 

It  was  resolved,  "  That  THE  INVENTORIES  AND  ACCOUNT  BOLLS 
I  OF  THE  BENEDICTINE  HOUSES  OR  CELLS  OF  JARROW  AND  MONK 
WEARMOUTH  be  edited  by  the  Rev.  JAMES  RAINE,  as  one  of  the 
books  of  the  Society  for  the  year  1854." 


J.  PEDDER,  Secretary. 


PREFACE. 


THE  members  of  the  Surtees  Society  and  the  public 
are  requested  to  consider  this  volume  as  a  further 
illustration  of  the  history  of  the  Benedictine  Institute 
in  England,  and  as  affording  much  local  and  general 
information,  of  the  most  varied  and  authentic  nature, 
upon  the  different  subjects  which  come  within  the 
scope  of  the  Society. 

With  the  history  of  the  Saxon  monasteries  of  Jarrow 
and  Monk  Wearmouth,  before  their  destruction  by 
the  Danes  in  the  ninth  century,  the  Editor  does  not 
profess  to  meddle ;  neither  does  the  nature  of  his 
subject  call  for  any  detail  with  respect  to  the  life  and 
learning  of  the  Venerable  Bede,  who  from  their  retire- 
ment shed  the  bright  light  of  his  erudition  over  the 
whole  civilised  world  of  his  period.*  Upon  these 
subjects  the  reader  is  referred  to  the  historical  works 
of  Bede  himself,  and  to  the  pages  of  Mr.  Surtees.  f 

The  history  of  the  second,  or  post-Norman,  monas- 
teries of  Jarrow  and  Wearmouth  commences  in  the 

*  It  will  be  seen  in  the  following  pages  how  the  monks  of  Jarrow  cherished 
the  memory  of  this  venerable  and  learned  man.  A  Life  of  Bede  was  the 
only  book,  with  the  exception  of  their  service  books,  which  finds  a  place  in 
their  inventories.  From  an  entry  in  the  inventory  of  1491  (p.  125)  it  would 
appear  that  in  the  same  volume  was  bound  a  Life  of  Benedict  Biscop,  the 
original  founder  of  the  two  Saxon  monasteries  of  which  Bede  was  the  distin- 
guished ornament. 

f  Vol.  ii.  pp.  2—6  and  66—69. 


PREFACE. 

pontificate  of  Walcher  bishop  of  Durham  (1072  —1080), 
during  which  period  Aldwin,  a  monk  of  Winchel- 
cumb,  with  Elfwi  and  Heinfrid,  his  associates,  monks 
of  Evesham,  migrated  northwards  on  foot,  having 
with  them  such  service-books  and  vestments  only  as 
could  be  carried  on  the  back  of  an  ass,  and  settled  at 
Monkchester,  now  Newcastle,  under  the  protection  of 
the  Earl  of  Northumberland.*  The  infant  colony  did 
not,  however,  remain  long  at  Monkchester.  The 
Bishop  of  Durham  above  mentioned  soon  afterwards 
tempted  them  to  cross  the  Tyne,  and  take  up  their 
abode  in  the  long-forsaken  convent  of  Jarrow,  the 
church  of  which,  as  Symeon  informs  us,  was  still 
standing,  but  without  a  roof.  This  defect  was  speedily 
remedied,  and  a  humble  edifice,  intended  to  serve  as 
a  refectory  and  dormitory,  was  erected  under  the  wing 
of  the  church.  As  the  number  of  the  monks  increased, 
the  bishop  enabled  them  to  rebuild  the  church  and 
its  offices,  by  the  endowment  of  six  neighbouring 
hamlets,  with  their  appurtenances  ;t  and  soon  after- 
wards the  same  prelate  conferred  upon  the  fraternity 
the  ruined  church  of  Monkwearmouth,  which  also 
was  speedily  restored;  and  at  length,  in  the  year 
1083,  the  whole  congregation,  partly  located  at  Jarrow 
and  partly  at  Wearmouth,  was  removed  by  Bishop 
William  de  Carilepho,  the  successor  of  Walcher,  and 
placed  in  his  newly  established  Benedictine  convent 
of  Durham. 

We  next  hear  of  the  two  monastic  institutions  of 
Jarrow   and   Monkwearmouth,    not    as    independent 

*  Sym.  Dunelm.  lib.  iii.  cap.  xxi.  f  Ibid. 


PREFACE.  IX 

houses,  but  as  cells  under  Durham,  their  mother 
church.  The  early  history  and  religious  associations 
of  the  two  places  led  almost  immediately  after  their 
second  desertion  to  a  third  establishment  within  their 
walls  of  certain  members  of  the  fraternity  by  whom 
they  had  been  a  while  before  forsaken,*  the  nature 
of  whose  dependence  upon  their  mother  house  of 
Durham,  and  the  revenues  which  they  were  permitted 
to  enjoy,  are  fully  developed  in  their  account  rolls, t 
and  in  the  documents  which  are  here  subjoined.  Each 
house  or  cell  was  governed  by  a  master  of  its  own, 
who,  with  one  solitary  fellow-monk,  or  occasionally, 
but  very  rarely,  with  two,  performed  the  daily  offices 
of  religion,  and  maintained  order  and  hospitality 
within  its  walls  4  Both  masters  and  monks  were 
removable  at  the  pleasure  of  the  Prior  of  Durham, 
and,  as  the  lists  in  the  sequel  prove,  this  pleasure  was 
exercised  frequently  and  freely.  Occasionally  a  sick 
monk  was  sent  to  one  of  the  cells  from  the  mother 
church,  or  some  other  of  its  dependencies,  for  the 
benefit  of  his  health,  and  this  regulation  was  also  the 
subject  of  a  solemn  writing  specifying  the  circum- 
stances of  the  case. 

*  "  Quia  de  dictis  locis  (Wermouth  et  Jarrow  in  marg.)  adducti  stint 
monachi  in  monasterium  Dunelm.  placuit  Willelmo  de  Karilepho  episcopo 
Dunelm.  et  Priori  Aldwyno  et  conventui  monachos  dicti  monasterii  ibidem 
habere."  Prior  Wessyngton's  History  of  the  Benedictine  Order,  MS.  D.  et 
C.  D.,  B.  iii.  30,  f.  37b. 

f  These  Account  Rolls  owe  their  origin  to  a  Constitution  of  the  church 
of  Durham  in  1235,  for  a  copy  of  which,  and  for  other  information  bearing 
upon  the  subject,  the  reader  is  referred  to  the  Preface  in  "  The  Priory  of 
Finchale,"  published  by  this  Society  in  1837. 

{  The  parochial  duties  were  performed  by  secular  priests,  of  which 
hereafter.

In  Dei  nomine,  Amen.     Constitutes  in  vestra  presencia,  pater 
reverende  domine  Johannes  Prior  ecclesise  Dunolmensis  ac  plu- 
rium  aliorum,  Ego  frater  Thomas  de  Graystanes  monachus  monas- 
terii  Dunolmensis,  ac  magister  domus  de  Jarow,  cellae  ejusdem 
monasterii,  protestor  in  hiis  scriptis  publice  et  expresse,   quod 
paratus  sum,   et  ero,   vobis  et  mandatis  vestris,  prout  de  jure 
teneor,  humiliter  et  reverenter  parere,  ac  in  omnibus  obedire,  ab 
hac  protestacione  non  recedendo,   sed  eidem,   quatenus  de  jure 
teneor,  firmiter  inherendo.     In  Dei  nomine,   Amen.     Quia  ego, 
frater  Thomas  de  Graystanes,  monachus  monasterii  Dunolmensis, 
ac  magister  domus  de  Jarow,   cellae  ejusdem  monasterii,  a  non 
nullis  intellexi,    quosdam   malivolos,   lucrum  proprium   nimium 
affectantes  vobis  maliciose  et  subdole  suggessisse,  me  quendam 
tractum  non  antesolitum  in  aqua  de  Tyne  incepisse,  temere  et 
injuste,  ac  ad  iirmam  aliis  dimisisse,  in  monasterii  Dunolmensis 
et  domus  de  Jarow,  cellae  ejusdem  monasterii,  prejudicium  et 
gravamen,  cum  tamen  lucide  patet,  et  patere  poterit  cuilibet  rem 
gestam  intueri  volenti,  ipsam  suggestionem  non  veritatem   sed 
mendacium  continere,  cum  tractus,  per  me  ex  consilio  confratrum 
et  secularium  inceptus,  non  cedet  in  dictorum  monasterii  et  cellse 
prejudicium  vel  gravamen,  sed  in  ipsorum  proficuum  non  modi- 
cum et  in  .  .  .  redditus  incrementum,  metuens  et  conjiciens  ex 
hiis  et  aliis  comminacionibus  verisimilibus  et  probabilibus  conjec- 
turis  prejudicialibus  cogitatis,  michi  et  statui  meo,  ac  monasterio 
Dunelmensi,  ac  domui  de  Jarow,  cellae  dicti  monasterii,  posse  in 
futurum  prejudicium  generari,  estimans  et  considerans  potius  esse 
pre venire  quam  preveniri,  et  tempore  occurrere  quam  post  causam 
vulneratam  remedium  quaerere,  volens  etiam  statum  meum,  monas-

votum.  Scripta  Dunelm.  secundo  die  mensis  Novembris  (1418).

in  cella  nostra  de  Jarowe,  quam  in  manibus  nostris  ad  tempus

quatenus  de  receptis  et  expensis  vestris  omnibus  et  singulis  pre- 
fatse  cellae  nobis,  cum  ad  id  vocati  fueritis,  plene  et  fideliter  com-

first,  written  by  the  Prior  of  Durham,  soon  after  the 
battle  of  Towton-field,  proves  that  the  Master  of  Jar- 
row,  a  dabbler  apparently  in  the  troubled  waters  of 
the  period,  was  labouring  under  a  charge  of  being

specified,  whether  master  or  monk-associate,  much  of  personal  history,  and  notices  of 
migration  from  cell  to  cell  from  the  completion  of  their  noviciate  to  their  grave.  On 
this  subject  it  may  suffice  to  refer  to  the  History  of  North  Durham  under  the  Cell  of 
Holy  Island,  p.  132.

favourable  to  the  house  of  Lancaster,  and  in  conse-

LITERA  MISSA  DOMINO  LE  FAUCOMBERGE.      (Reg.  III.  parv.  f.  97  b. ) 

Eight  noble  and  worthie  lord,  I  recommende  me  in  my  most 
humble  wise  unto  your  Lordshipp.  And,  for  as  much  as  I  am 
enformed  that  ye  are  heuy  lord  vnto  my  brothir  the  Maistre  of 
Jarowe,  by  enformacion  yiffen  vnto  you  by  George  of  Lumley, 
the  which  I  wold  noght  haue  tristid  vnto  him,  I  wolde  beseche 
your  Lordshipp  that,  yf  ye  think  it  to  be  don,  he  might  come 
vnto  your  presence  for  his  declaracion ;  other  elles  that  it  might 
like  your  good  grace  so  to  provide  for  him  that  he  may  surely 
ride  and  goo  in  the  cuntry,  whar  him  liketh,  for  the  wele  and 
profett  of  his  place ;  for  I  dar  say  and  make  it  goode  that  he  is, 
and  hidirto  hath  ben,  a  trew  man,  wel willing  unto  Kyng  Edward, 
my  Lorde  of  Warwike,  unto  you,  and  to  all  that  belongith  vnto 
that  party.  And  our  Lord  Jhesu  Crist  haue  yow  euermore  in 
his  blissid  kepyng.  "Writen  at  Duresme,  the  fyfte  day  of  July. 
[1461.] 

By  your  trew  and  continuell  bedeman, 
JOHN,  the  Priour  of  Duresme. 

To  the  right  noble  and  worthy 
Lord  the  Lord  Faucomberge. 


LITERA  MISSA  DOMINO  WILLELMO  BOWES.      (Reg.  III.  parv.  99  b  J 

Right  worshipfull  Sir,  I  recommende  me  unto  you.  And  for- 
asmuch as  I  am  enformed  now  of  late  that  ye  desire  to  have  in 
fermyng  the  tendecorne  of  Hilton  for  certeyn  yeres,  the  which 
my  brother  of  Wermouth,  to  whome  it  belongith,  may  noght 
forgoo  in  no  maner  of  wise  withoutyn  grete  hurt  of  him  and  of 
the  said  place;  for  it  hath  ben  said  to  me  by  indifferent  persones, 


PREFACE.  XX1U 

afor  this  tyme,  by  cause  of  such  manner  of  dimissions  hade  afor 
tyme  he  stondith  now  destitute  of  almaner  of  corne,  and  byethe 
it  daily  at  the  derrest  price,  to  him  and  to  the  said  place  grete 
hurte  and  hinderaunce,  for  in  defaute  of  stray  (straw)  his  places 
are  unrepareld,  his  catell  famyshed,  and  his  lande  unmaynerd 
(unmanured).  Wharuppon,  I,  consideryng  the  grete  hurtis  that 
growethe  daily  unto  the  said  place  by  such  maner  of  dimissions, 
at  the  counsaill  of  my  brethir  hafe  gifFen  him  in  charge,  uppon 
parell  that  may  fall,  to  make  noon  heraftir  withoute  my  speciall 
licence.  Wharefore,  this  mater  by  your  hye  wisdome  dewly  con- 
siderd,  I  beseke  you  to  be  his  goode  maistre,  and  to  suffre  him 
peasebly  forto  occupye  and  rejoyce  the  said  tende  unto  his  most 
profett;  for,  in  case  that  it  shulde  be  lett  to  ferme  to  any  man,  it 
is  my  will  and  his  both  that  ye  shulde  have  it  a  for  any  othir. 
[1461.] 

Among  the  numerous  papers  connected  with  the 
suit  between  the  Prior  of  Durham  and  Staynton  the 
Chaplain  of  Hilton,  noticed  in  the  Appendix,  p.  243, 
are  letters  from  the  former  to  the  Lord  High  Chan- 
cellor, the  Bishop  of  Durham,  Lord  Faucomberge,  the 
law  advisers  of  the  convent  in  London  and  Home,  &c. 
Of  these  a  place  may  be  allowed  for  two,  one  addressed 
by  the  Prior  to  his  proctor  at  Rome,  and  the  other  by 
the  Bishop  of  Durham  to  the  person  who  served  him 
there  in  the  same  capacity,  both  acting  under  a  gene- 
ral retainer.  Even  if  Staynton  had  been  engaged  in 
a  "righteous"  cause  it  would  have  been  hard  for  him 
to  have  contended  with  success  against  such  interest 
as  was  brought  to  bear  against  him.  In  the  Bishop's 
letter  there  seems  to  be  a  recommendation  of  a  packed 
commission.  It  does  not  appear  what  became  of 
Staynton;  but  Creswell,  his  successor,  entered  into  a 


XXIV  PREFACE. 


solemn  bond  not  to  prosecute  the  suit  commenced  by 
him  in  the  court  of  Rome. 


LITERA  MISSA  M.  JOHANNI  LAXE.      (Reg.  III.  parv.  99.) 

Eight  worshipfull  Sr,  I  recommende  me  unto  you,  acertifiyng 
you  by  thies  presents  how  that  the  xv.  day  of  July  was  affixt 
uppon  our  kirke  dure  a  cedule  unsealid,  inhibityng  me  and  my 
brethir  nothing  forto  attempt  against  oure  adversary,  Sr  Eobert 
Staynton,  prest  of  the  chapell  of  Hilton,  honging  his  appell  in 
the  Courte  of  Kome,  and  over  that,  cityng  us  forto  appere  at 
Kome  the  iiijxx  day  next  aftir,  to  make  answer  unto  him  in  the 
same,  whareof  I  marvell  gretly,  considering  the  speciall  writyng 
that  I  and  Doctour  Lilford  wrote  vnto  you  a  for  tyme  in  the 
same  mater,  that  sich  thing  shuld  be  purchessid  or  passefurth 
agaynst  us,  ye  beyng  our  speciall  proctur,  abidyng  in  the  Courte, 
and  wold  lett  us  have  no  knawlech  therof  by  writing  in  dew 
tyme.  Neverthelesse,  sith  that  it  is  so,  I  pray  you  hertfully  that 
ye  will  take  the  mater  to  hert  affectuously,  in  withstondyng  of 
his  grete  malice;  and  aftir  your  hie  wisdome  and  the  effecte  of 
the  processe  hade  at  Yorke  in  the  same  mater  a  for  tyme,  and  of 
other  evidence  that  I  sende  with  thies,  ye  wold  in  that  that  in 
you  is,  provide  us  of  a  competent  remedy  as  law  will,  as  my  full 
trist  is  in  you,  and  as  I  may  deserve  it  unto  you  in  tyme  to  come : 
and  oure  Lord  Jhesu  Christ  have  you  evermore  in  his  blissid 
kepyng.  Writen  at  Durham,  the  xxij.  day  of  July  [1461]. 
Your  awn  frende, 

JOHN,  the  Priour  of  Duresme. 


LITERA  DOMINI  DUNELMENSIS  EPISCOPI  DIRECTA  M.  THOM^E  HOPE. 

(Reg.  III.  parv.  101. ) 

Eight  trusty  and  welbeloved  frende,  I  commaunde  me  unto 
you:  and  forasmuch  as  my  right  worshipfull  and  welbeloved 
brother  the  Priour  of  Duresme  is  citide  to  appere  at  Eome  afor 


PREFACE.  XXV 

an  auditour  called  Bernard  Romia,  or  his  surrogate,  to  answer  in 
a  mater  of  apeill  at  the  instaunce  of  Sr  Robert  Staynton,  prest  of 
Hilton,  I  pray  you  as  hertly  as  I  can,  that,  considering  the  trouith 
and  goode  willes  that  my  said  brother  the  priour  and  the  chirch 
of  Duresme  hath  put  to  the  King  our  Soverayn  Lord,  the  which 
will  write  specially  unto  our  holy  fader  for  thaim,  that  ye  wold 
shew  unto  thaim  your  speciall  favour  and  helpe  in  the  mater 
aforsaid,  and  that  ye  wold  help  to  gete  a  delegacy  direct  to 
certayn  persones  whose  names  shalbe  send  to  you,  and  that  ye 
will  labur  in  this  mater  effectually  as  my  speciall  trist  is  in  you. 
In  the  which  labur  ye  shall  noght  only  deserve  speciall  thanke 
and  good  will  of  me,  but  also  ye  shalbe  sure  of  the  goode  willes 
of  my  said  brothre  the  priour  and  all  the  chirch,  and  also  be 
rewarded  for  your  labur ;  and  moreover  I  pray  you  of  all  maters 
comonde  betweyn  you  and  me  to  sende  me  alse  hasty  word  as  ye 
may,  and  specially  in  the  mater  touching  Syrnon  Noree,  and  I 
counsaill  you  to  spede  the  Kinges  materes  in  all  the  hast  ye  may. 
And  the  blissid  Trinite  have  you  in  his  blissid  kepyng.  Writen 
at  Aukland,  the  ix.  day  of  Septembre  [1461]. 

Your  frend, 

LAUR.  bysshop  of  Duresme. 
Dilecto  et  fideli  amico  magistro 
THOM.E  HOPE. 

The  cells  of  Jarrow  and  Wermouth  shared  the  fate 
of  such  monasteries  as  could  not  boast  of  a  revenue 
amounting  to  above  200/.  per  annum,  and  being  dis- 
solved by  the  act  of  27  Hen.  VIII.  their  possessions 
are  now  held  by  laymen,  and,  as  is  usual  in  such  cases, 
their  churches,  having  been  parochial  as  well  as  con- 
ventual, serve  now  as  parish  churches,  but  with  no 
suitable  endowment.  In  the  Roll  for  1536-7,  p.  136, 
we  have  account  of  a  payment  of  61.  to  Doctor 
Hynemers  for  his  expenses  in  London.  Hynemers 


XXVI  PREFACE. 

was  the  Bishop's  Yicar  General,*  and  the  object  of  his 
journey  was  to  plead  for  the  cells  in  connection  with 
Durham,  it  may  be  by  a  bribe,  and  to  save  them  if 
possible  from  confiscation.  But  his  errand  was  fruit- 
less. 

A  few  words  may  be  necessary  with  respect  to  the 
architectural  features  of  these  two  churches.  In  both 
cases  there  is  much  of  history,  and  much  to  engage 
the  attention  of  the  student.  And  first  of  Jarrow. 

The  first  thing  to  be  mentioned  is  the  inscription,  of 
undoubted  authenticity,  and  still  in  good  preservation, 
recording  the  dedication  of  the  Saxon  church  in  the 
year  685,  placed  over  the  chancel  arch  in  1782,  having 
been  previously  in  the  north  wall  :— 


DEDICATIO  BASILICAE 
SCI  PAVLI  VIIII  KL  MAI 
ANNO  XV  EGEBIDI  KEG 
CEOLFB1DI  ABB  EIVSDEMQ 
Q-  ECCLES  DO  AVCTOEE 
CONDITOBJS  ANNO  IIII 

Jarrow  is  perhaps  the  only  church  in  the  kingdom

it  till  the  year  1069  or  1070,  when  it  afforded  a  shelter 
for  the  night  to  the  Durham  ecclesiastics  who  were 
escaping  to  Lindisfarne  with  the  body  of  St.  Cuthhert 
in  dread  of  the  Conqueror.  Shortly  afterwards,  in 
1075,  Walcher  Bishop  of  Durham,  the  unfortunate 
prelate  eventually  burnt  to  death  in  Gateshead  church, 
gives,  as  has  been  already  stated  above,  to  the  Monk 
Aldwin  and  his  two  associates,  "  the  monastery  of  the 
blessed  Paul  the  Apostle,  built  of  old  by  Benedict  the 
Abbot  in  Gyruum,  which,  its  walls  alone  standing 
without  a  roof,  had  scarcely  retained  any  mark  of  its 
antient  grandeur.  Upon  which  walls  placing  a  roof 
of  rough  timber  and  hay,  they  began  to  perform  divine 
service  in  the  edifice,  and  having  constructed  a  small 
cottage  under  its  walls  in  which  to  sleep  and  take  their 
meals,  they  led  a  life  of  poverty,  living  upon  the  alms 
of  the  religious."*  This  is  all  intelligible,  but  then 
towards  the  end  of  the  same  chapter  we  have  this  fur- 
ther statement :  "When  he  (the  Bishop)  saw  the  monks 
wishful  to  rebuild  the  church  itself,  and  the  ruined 
monastic  dwelling-places,  he  gave  to  them  the  vill  of 
Jarrow  with  its  appendages,  viz.  Preston,"  &c.  The 
question  now  arises,  did  the  monks  so  rebuild  their 
church,  and  do  the  earlier  portions  of  the  fabric  as 
they  now  remain  belong  to  that  period,  thus  taking 
their  rank  among  the  very  earliest  of  our  Norman 
structures,  or  may  we  go  back  to  the  seventh  century 
for  the  date  of  their  masonry  ?  In  the  opinion  of  the 
Editor,  the  Saxon  date  cannot  be  maintained.  The

translation.

tower  can  boast  of  no  feature  decidedly  Saxon  accord- 
ing to  modern  discrimination,  save  two  portions  of  a 
Saxon  cross  of  fret-work  in  its  northern  outside  face, 
inserted  at  random  as  common  building- stones  at  the 
time  of  its  construction,  whenever  that  took  place, — a 
decided  proof  of  subsequent  date,  if  others  were  want- 
ing ;  and  further,  the  peculiar  features  of  the  chancel, 
upon  which  some  zealous  Saxonists  rely,  do  not  differ 
materially  from  other  buildings  of  early  but  post- 
conquest  date,  such  as  the  base  of  Hatfield's  great 
hall  in  Durham  Castle,  and  the  windows  discovered 
during  the  reparations  in  1847  of  the  church  of  St. 
Mary  in  the  South  Bailey,  a  fabric  most  unquestionably 
not  antecedent  to  the  eleventh  century.  The  monastic 
buildings  of  Jarrow  are  in  ruin  and  confusion,  but 
neither  can  they  lay  claim  to  a  date  anterior  to  the 
Conquest.  There  is  a  narrow  door- way,  with  its  head 
constructed  of  two  thin  stones,  set  thus  /V>  but  the 
accompanying  characteristics  prove  this  arrangement 
to  be  capricious  or  accidental.  In  the  chancel  there 
is  some  excellent  stall- work  in  black  oak,  elaborately 
carved,  and  belonging  apparently  to  the  earlier  part  of 
the  fifteenth  century.  The  various  building  notices 
in  the  Rolls  will  not  fail  to  attract  the  attention  of 
the  architectural  reader.* 

*  The  present  state  of  the  church  of  Jarrow,  and  its  requirements,  parochially  and 
architecturally  considered,  will  be  best  understood  by  the  following  Report,  to  which  the 
Editor  willingly  gives  a  place  in  the  hope  that  the  subject  may  attract  the  favourable 
attention  of  those  who  take  an  interest  in  church  restoration  and  enlargement.  The 
Venerable  Bede  ought  not  to  plead  in  vain,  and  Mr.  Scott  is  well  known  as  one  of  the 
best  informed  and  most  judicious  of  our  ecclesiastical  architects.  The  population  of 
Jarrow  is  not  far  short  of  5,000,  consisting  almost  entirely  of  pitmen  and  day- 


PREFACE. 


XXIX 


The  church  of  "Wermouth  (Monk  Wearmouth)  must 
next  be  mentioned.  And  here  again  we  have  the  same 
early  history — its  foundation  in  Saxon  times,  and  its 
sisterhood  with  Jarrow,  until  the  destruction  of  the 

labourers,  with  church  accommodation  for  only  320.     The  revenues  of  the  monks  of 
Jarrow,  as  has  been  already  stated,  are  in  the  hands  of  laymen : — 

"  REPORT. — RESTORATION  OF  JARROW  CHURCH. — The  remains  of  this  monastery,  so 
famous  as  the  residence  of  the  Venerable  Bede,  present  now  a  melancholy  picture.  The 
contrast  they  offer  between  the  past  and  the  present  is  peculiarly  characteristic.  In  old 
times,  while  inhabitants  were  few,  the  provision  for  the  worship  of  God  was  the  leading 
feature  of  the  place.  Now,  however,  that  its  rural  beauties  are  destroyed  by  collieries 
and  manufactures,  and  its  retirement  by  crowded  habitations,  not  only  have  the  resi- 
dences of  the  ministers  of  religion  fallen  into  scarcely  distinguishable  ruin  (not  even 
an  habitation  for  one  clergyman  being  left),  but  the  church  itself  threatens  to  follow 
them  in  decay,  and  its  dimensions  are  not  only  quite  insufficient  for  the  vastly 
increased  population,  but  are  probably  much  less  than  when  there  were  so  few  inha- 
bitants to  worship  in  it. — The  only  portions  of  the  church  which  belong  to  the  ancient 
structure  are  the  chancel  and  the  central  tower. — The  nave  has  been  rebuilt  in  modern 
times  in  the  most  wretched  and  incongruous  style. — The  chancel  and  tower  are  of  a 
very  early  date.  I  will  not  venture  to  pronounce  upon  their  age,  but  they  are  gene- 
rally supposed  to  be  of  ante-Norman  date.  Be  this  as  it  may,  they  are  decidedly  very 
early  Romanesque,  and  the  tower  in  particular  is  of  very  peculiar  and  characteristic 
design,  widely  differing  from  the  usual  form  of  Norman  towers. — It  retains,  almost 
untouched,  its  original  form  and  details;  while  the  chancel,  probably  of  the  same  date, 
shows  its  original  features  less  distinctly,  its  small  Romanesque  windows  placed  high 
in  the  wall  being  blocked  up  and  large  Gothic  windows  inserted,  while  its  high  roof, 
clearly  marked  against  the  tower,  has  made  way  for  one  absolutely  flat. — The  upper 
portions  of  the  tower,  though  a  good  deal  decayed  and  weather-beaten  by  the  storms  of 
some  eight  centuries,  still  appear  pretty  strong  and  substantial.  Such,  however,  is  far 
from  being  the  case  below.  The  four  piers,  on  which  the  tower  is  supported,  appear 
to  have  long  since  partially  given  way,  and  huge  rents  in  the  walls  and  arches  to  have 
followed.  The  evil  was  partially  remedied  by  adding  three  large  buttresses,  and  by 
thickening  the  lower  part  of  the  wall  which  faces  the  nave.  To  the  fourth  pier,  how- 
ever, no  buttress  was  added,  though  on  examining  the  wall  and  arch  by  removing  the 
plastering,  I  find  it  to  be  in  a  fearful  state  of  disintegration,  so  much  so  as  to  excite 
one's  wonder  that  it  has  not  long  since  finally  given  way. — I  cannot  consider  that  in 
the  present  condition  of  the  tower  there  is  security  against  its  yielding  to  any  storm 
from  the  south-west — its  north-eastern  limb  being  shattered  and  utterly  unsupported. — 
This  cause  of  danger  ought  without  delay  to  be  remedied,  which  would  be  a  work 
requiring  considerable  care  and  involving  a  good  deal  of  ^ot. — At  the  same  time  the 
foundations  of  the  chancel  walls  should  be  examined  and  underpinned,  and  a  buttress 
added  in  at  least  one  place,  where  there  is  a  serious  bulge. — The  required  enlargement 
of  the  nave  will  be  best  obtained  by  the  addition  of  a  north  aisle,  and,  perhaps,  also 
by  a  slight  extension  to  the  west.  As  it  would  be  pedantic  to  attempt  to  make  this 


XXX  PREFACE. 

two  by  the  Danes,  and  afterwards  their  reunion  under 
Aldwin  and  his  southern  companions  in  1075.  For 
what  then  took  place  at  "Wermouth  Symeon  must 
again  be  our  guide.  Of  the  church,  as  he  informs  us,* 
the  walls  alone  were  standing,  and  they  half  in  ruins ; 
and  we  further  learn  from  him  that  the  fabric  was 
entirely  choked  with  trees,  briars  and  thorns  (the 
growth  of  208  years),  which  the  monks  cleared  away, 
placing  upon  it  a  roof  "  as  it  is  now  seen,"  and  busied 
themselves  in  restoring  it  for  Divine  service.  The 
words  "  as  it  is  now  seen"  (quale  hodie  cernitur)  would 
no  doubt  have  been  of  great  importance  in  a  question 
of  Saxon  or  Norman  architecture,  if  any  portion  of  the 
church  of  which  Symeon  is  manifestly  writing  had 
remained  to  the  present  day,  especially  when  it  is 
remembered  that  he  was  born  about  the  year  1060, 

addition  in  the  rude  style  of  the  early  features,  it  should  be  in  some  simple  and  bold 
but  unpretending  variety  of  Gothic  architecture,  and  the  present  nave  should  have  a 
new  roof,  and  be  in  other  respects  conformed  to  the  same  character. — The  present 
incongruous  vestry  should  be  rebuilt,  perhaps  in  such  a  form  as  to  make  a  small 
transept  to  that  side  of  the  tower,  as  it  appears  intended  to  receive  some  such  adjunct, 
and  it  would  tend  to  strengthen  its  piers.  It  might,  perhaps,  be  so  managed  as  to 
facilitate  the  passage  of  the  voice  from  the  altar  into  the  new  aisle,  which  would  be 
somewhat  obstructed  by  the  small  size  of  the  openings  through  the  tower. — There  are 
arches,  now  walled  up,  which  appear  to  have  opened  from  the  chamber  above  the 
vaulting  of  the  tower  eastward  and  westward  into  the  chancel  and  nave,  forming  that 
chamber  into  something  equivalent  to  a  rood-loft.  One  of  these  arches  is  much  shat- 
tered, but  if  restored  they  might  be  opened,  and  the  organ  be  placed  in  this  position, 
which  would  be  very  suitable  for  it.— I  would  not  do  away  with  the  present  Gothic 
windows  of  the  chancel,  but  I  think  that  the  little  Romanesque  windows  which  remain 
should  be  opened  out  and  glazed.— The  ancient  door  should  also  be  opened  and 
restored.— The  present  flat  roof  of  the  chancel  should  be  removed,  and  a  new  one 
formed,  suited  to  the  original  roof  mark  against  the  tower.— I  fear  the  cost  of  the 
works  I  have  recommended  will  not  be  less  than  £1,400  or  £1,500,  as  so  considerable 
an  extension  is  required  to  meet  the  wants  of  the  population.

PREFACE.  XXXI 

and  at  all  events  was  alive  and  took  a  part  in  the 
translation  of  St.  Cuthbert  in  1104 ;  but  the  present 
church  can  lay  no  claim  to  such  antiquity,  being  of 
various  dates  during  its  existence  as  a  cell.  It  is  with 
the  tower  alone  that  we  have  to  do,  and  here  again 
we  have  an  edifice  apparently  of  the  most  decided  early 
Norman  character,  earlier,  however,  as  it  would  appear, 
than  Jarrow,  and  thus  much  might  have  been  expected 
from  Symeon's  narrative.  What  the  monks  did  at 
Monkwearmouth  they  did  immediately ;  but  at  Jarrow, 
as  it  may  be  inferred  from  that  authority,  some  time 
elapsed  before  they  began  to  rebuild  their  church  in 
earnest.  The  tower  of  Monkwearmouth  is  so  thickly 
covered  with  rough-cast  as  to  prevent  an  examination 
of  its  masonry;  but  its  windows  remain  in  their  ancient 
state,  and  they  are,  in  the  opinion  of  one  whose  expe- 
rience in  such  questions  is  most  worthy  of  attention, 
merely  of  an  early  Norman  character.  Besides,  there 
is  an  external  string-course  of  the  usual  Norman  work- 
manship. 

And  not  only  do  the  monks  of  Wermouth  seem  to 
have  built  their  church  anew,  but  upon  a  different  site- 
In  the  year  1360  (p.  159)  there  was  standing  an  edifice 
which  was  called  the  old  church,  in  which  was  stored 
up  a  stack  of  tithe-barley ;  and  again,  nearly  a  century 
afterwards  (p.  241),  the  same  building,  called,  in  plain 
English  "  the  aide  kirke,"  was  still  in  existence,  partly 
used  as  a  stable  and  partly  as  a  storehouse  for  hay. 
Now  what  could  this  old  church  of  1360  have  been, 
except  one  which  had  been  superseded  by  a  more  recent 
structure,  and  to  what  period  could  this  more  recent 


PREFACE. 

structure  have  owed  its  origin  except  to  the  second 
settlement  in  1075  ? 

But  as  at  Jarrow  we  have  a  striking  memorial  of  its  1 
Saxon  period,  so  also  carved  stones  have  been  found  at 
Monkwearmouth,  which  may  perhaps  have  ornamented 
its  original  church.  About  twenty-five  years  ago,  in 
removing  a  mass  of  rubbish  and  loose  stones  within 
the  present  tower,  on  its  eastern  side,  a  little  below  the 
floor,  a  small  arch  was  discovered,  leading  as  it  appeared 


into  a  vault,  too  confined,  however,  in  space  for  a  grave, 
except  for  a  child,  more  perhaps  resembling  a  recess 
or  closet  cut  out  in  the  wall.  On  each  side  of  this 
aperture  was  a  short  pillar  or  baluster  of  magnesian 
limestone,  about  twenty-two  inches  in  height  and  eight 
inches  in  diameter  at  the  head,  ornamented  by  a  lathe

XXX111 


after  the  manner  represented  in  the  engraving,  with 
dowel  or  pivot  holes  at  each  end.  In  both  stones  there 
is  a  small  roughly-formed  groove  or  gap  in  the  side 
from  top  to  bottom,  of  a  later  date,  as  if  cut  away  to 
admit  of  their  being  let  in  to  the  corner  of  the  wall 
where  they  were  found.  Of  these  balusters  one  rested 
upon  another  stone,  of  which  also  a  drawing  is  here 


>iven,  but  the  Editor  does  not  venture  to  express  his 
opinion  with  respect  to  the  emblematical  meaning  of 
its  carvings.* 

It  must  be  stated  that  two  stones,  of  precisely  the 
same  character*  and  dimensions  as  the  balusters  above 
described,  are  still  serving  as  jambs,  as  far  as  they  ex- 
tend, to  a  closed-up  doorway  of  early  character  in  the

XXXIV  PREFACE. 

inside  of  the  same  tower,  in  its  base  story  and  western 
wall.  The  door-way,  having  a  plain  and  rudely  executed 
semicircular  head,  measures  5  feet  8  inches  in  width, 
and  its  sides,  except  as  far  as  the  above  carved  stones 
extend,  are  of  plain  masonry. 

It  may  be  added  that  there  is  standing  in  an  upright 
position  up  to  its  knees  in  the  ground,  against  the 
outside  of  the  tower,  a  well-carved  effigy  of  a  monk, 
commemorating  doubtless  either  the  Master  William 
Trollop,  or  the  Master  John  Rypon.  The  former  was 
buried  here  in  1407,  and  the  latter  in  1413.  This 
monument  was  not  long  ago  ejected  from  its  original 
place  in  the  chancel. 

In  other  respects  the  church  of  Monkwearmouth 
contains  nothing  to  engage  the  attention  of  the  archi- 
tect or  antiquary,  but  very  much  indeed,  with  sorrow 
it  must  be  confessed,  to  excite  feelings  of  deep  pain  at 
its  condition  in  every  way  as  a  place  of  Divine  worship. 

The  Surtees  Society  has  repeatedly  had  occasion  to 
express  its  thanks  to  the  Dean  and  Chapter  of  Durham 
for  access  to  the  historical  treasures  in  their  possession. 
The  present  volume  adds  to  its  obligations.  Every 
record  in  the  following  pages  is  obtained,  by  the  per- 
mission of  that  body,  from  the  stores  of  their  treasury.

*  The  Editor  has  so  far  proceeded  upon  the  plan  of  printing  these  records  without 
abridgment,  although,  as  it  will  have  been  observed,  there  has  been  much  of  repe- 
tition. But  it  must  be  remembered  that  at  this  early  period  the  slightest  variation  in 
spelling,  or  mode  of  expression ,  in  the  annual  description  of  any  article  in  an  Inven- 
tory, or  any  word  whatever  in  use  at  the  time,  is  frequently  of  itself  sufficient  to  settle 
e  meaning  of  the  term  for  ever,  and  important  doubts  of  this  kind  have  been  already 

numerous  instances  removed  in  the  preceding  pages.     The  items  of  receipt  and  ex-

XXXVI.  M.CCC.LVII.  ASC.  AD  F.  S.  MATTH'.  FRATER  RICARDUS  DE  BYKER- 

TON  MAGISTER. 

Recepta.     Et  de  vij  s.  ix  d.  r.  de  uno  equo  vendito   domino 
Johanni  Jakes  capellano.     Et  de  Ixxij  s.  r.   de  lana  et  pellibus 

penditure  are  now,  it  must  further  be  remarked,  becoming  more  uniform  and  settled. 
The  plan  which  the  Editor  therefore  proposes  to  adopt  is  as  follows  :  to  print,  without 
any  abridgment,  every  STATUS  or  INVENTORY,  strictly  so  called,  as  their  importance 
increases,  as  they  diminish  in  number,  and  also,  at  the  least,  one  compotus  or  account 
roll  in  every  fifteen  years.  The  number  may  be  increased  from  circumstances,  but  never 
diminished,  and  of  those  of  the  intermediate  years  every  one  which  has  been  preserved 
will  be  noticed,  with  such  extracts  as  bear  upon  this  small  fraternity  itself,  the  fluc- 
tuation of  its  income,  and  its  intercourse  with  the  neighbourhood.  These  extracts  will 
in  general  be  given  in  the  words  of  the  roll,  but  a  more  abridged  plan  still  may 
eventually  be  adopted.  No  word  or  thing,  however,  in  which  any  member  of  the 
Society  can  take  an  interest  will  be  omitted.  The  words  of  ordinary  occurrence  in 
each  Roll,  such  as  receptis,  venditis,  emptis,  infra  tempus  compoti,  per  idem  tempus,  &c. 
will  in  general  be  expressed  by  the  initial  letter  of  each  word,  after  it  has  once  occured 
at  length  in  the  document,  and  the  word  Item,  of  such  frequent  occurrence  in  the 
expense  part  of  the  Roll,  will  be  in  general  omitted  entirely.

altera  medietas  pertinet  ad  officium  Bursarii  Dunelm.  Et  de  xv  s. 

*  King  Henry  the  Sixth  was  the  guest  of  Bishop  Neville  in  the  Castle  of  Durham 
from  the  26th  to  the  30th  of  September,  1448,  and  the  monks  of  Jarrow  appear  to 
have  contributed  capons,  rabbits,  and  wine,  towards  his  entertainment  there  or  in  the 
Convent.  We  must  not,  I  think,  infer  from  the  entry  that  the  King  paid  a  personal 
visit  to  Jarrow.  For  some  interesting  particulars  of  this  royal  visit  the  reader  is  re- 
ferred to  "  A  Description  of  the  Ancient  Monuments,  &c.  within  the  Monastical  Church 
of  Durham,"  published  by  this  Society  in  1842.

Remanentia.  Idem  respondet  de  xvj  s.  ij  d.  de  remanentibus 
ultimi  compoti  precedentis,  ut  patet  in  pede  ejusdem.  Summa 
patet.  Et  de  xxvj  s.  ij  d.  de  redditu  assisaa  de  Schele},  Jarowe  et 
Monkton  de  terminis  Pentecostes  et  Martini.  Et  de  xl  s.  de 
Stanyar  et  Utvar  de  eisdem  terminis,  sic  dimissis  hoc  anno.  Et 
de  iij  s.  receptis  de  firma  medietatis  de  Pulyar,  et  non  plus,  quia 
altera  medietas  pertinet  ad  officium  bursarii.  Et  de  xvj  s.  v  d.  r. 
de  decimis  piscariarum  ex  australi  parte  aquae  de  Tyne.  De  pis-

*  Of  the  greater  part  of  this  STATUS,  the  last  which  will  present  itself,  the  original 
draft  upon  paper  has  been  preserved,  drawn  up  apparently  by  Swan,  the  receding 
master.  As  the  draft  is  full  of  character,  and  contains  many  items  omitted  by  the  more 
courtly  scribe,  and  others  detailed  in  a  more  homely  but  characteristic  way,  supplying, 
besides,  as  it  does,  many  items  affected  by  time  or  damp  in  the  document  before  us,  it 
is  printed  literatim  after  the  present  number.

CXLYH.  COMPOTUS  DOMINI  JOHANNIS  HAMSTERLY  MAGISTRI  SIVE  CUS- 
TODIS  CELL^E  DE  JAROWE  A  FESTO  PENTECOSTES  ANNO  DOMINI 
MILLESIMO  CCCC.  NONAGESIMO  QUINTO  USQUE  IDEM  FESTUM  ANNO 
DOMINI  MILLESIMO  CCCC.  NONAGESIMO  VJ. 

Kemanentia  nulla.  Recepta.  Sed  respondet  de  xxvij  s.  viij  d. 
receptis  de  redditu  assisse  de  Sheles,  Jarowe,  et  Monketon.  Et 
de  xxxiij  s.  iiij  d.  r.  de  Staneyare  et  Uttyng  cum  decimis  eorun- 
dem,  sic  dimissis  Roberto  Nelson  et  Willelmo  Nelson  hoc  anno. 
Et  de  ^ .  .  .  s.  r.  pro  firma  medietatis  de  Pulyar,  et  non  plus, 
quia  alia_medietas  pertinet  officio  bursarii.  Et  de  xvj  s.  r.  de 
decimis  piscariarum  ex  australi  parte  aquae  de  Tyne.  De  piscariis

APPENDIX. 


JARROW. 

P.  4.  The  Fix  of  St.  Hilda. — A  box,  called  in  subsequent  entries 
(pp.  76  and  78)  the  Fix  of  the  Holy  Rood,  and  placed  for  the  purpose  of 
an  alms-box  in  the  chapel  of  St.  Hild  at  Shields.  There  was  a  similar 
box,  called  the  Box  of  St.  Peter,  in  the  church  of  Wermouth.  Vide 
p.  195,  &c. 

P.  6.  William  de  Taunfeld. — This  person,  who  had  been  Prior  of 
Durham  from  1307  to  1313,  resigned  his  office  in  the  latter  year, 
"  senectutis  et  impotentise  sentiens  incommoda,"  and  received  by  a  grant 
of  his  convent  the  Cell  of  Jarrow  for  his  maintenance,  with  leave  to 
reside  at  his  pleasure  in  the  manor  house  of  Wardeley,  and  occasion- 
ally, but  not  for  a  longer  period  than  five  or  six  days,  in  the  monastery 
of  Durham.  The  deed  of  endowment  for  the  retiring  dignitary  contains 
some  interesting  particulars,  especially  a  provision,  that  in  case  Jarrow 
should  be  destroyed  during  any  hostile  incursion  on  the  part  of  Scotland, 
the  ex-dignitary  should  be  accommodated  with  apartments  in  the  monas- 
tery at  home  sufficiently  large  for  a  fellow-monk,  a  valet,  and  four 
boys.  For  the  document  itself  see  "  Archseolog.  ^Eliana,"  I.  115.  The 
heading  of  the  Roll  No.  VI.  in  p.  6  above,  proves  that  the  ex-prior  died 
in  January  1313-4,  having  survived  his  resignation  not  much  more 
than  half  a  year.  At  p.  9  we  have  mention  made  of  a  quarter  of  wheat 
distributed  to  the  poor  upon  the  day  of  his  burial ;  and,  from  the  cir- 
cumstance that  two  quarters  of  oats  were  consumed  by  horses  on  the 
same  day,  there  appears  to  have  been  a  large  concourse  of  people  at 
his  funeral.  He  was  no  doubt  interred  at  Jarrow.  The  circumstance 
of  the  prior's  residence  at  Jarrow  will  account  for  the  various  and  irregular 
Account  Rolls  during  his  period  of  occupation.  Page  10  proves  that 
he  was  a  benefactor  to  the  Cell.  The  other  monks  who  appear  to  have 
died  and  to  have  been  buried  at  Jarrow,  were,  Simon  de  Derlington,  in 
1368  (p.  51),  and  John  Durham,  the  master,  1442  (p.  108).  The 
monks  buried  at  Wermouth  were  William  Trollop,  in  1407,  p.  189, 
and  John  Rypon,  the  master,  in  1413,  p.  191. 

P.  15.  Comes  de  Adthetill  It  is  matter  of  history  that  David  Earl 
of  Athol,  appointed  High  Constable  of  Scotland  in  1312,  was  soon 
afterwards  deprived  of  his  honours  and  estates  in  consequence  of  having 
deserted  the  cause  of  Robert  Bruce,  and  it  is  certain  that  he  took  refuge 


234  APPENDIX. 

in  England.  Did  he  become  for  a  while  an  inmate  of  the  house  of 
Jarrow  as  a  lodger,  or  did  he  borrow  100s.  of  the  fraternity  ?  The 
debt  was  never  paid.  In  1330  (p.  19)  we  find  the  Earl  dead  and 
the  money  hopeless  The  item  is  however  entered  in  the  Roll  for 
some  time  afterwards. 

P.  37.  The  Chaplain  of  St.  Hild.  It  is  proved  by  the  Rolls  that  the 
monks  of  Jarrow  were  owners,  under  the  monastery  of  Durham,  among 
other  things,  of  certain  tithes  within  the  parish,  that  the  parochial 
duties  of  Jarrow  itself  were  performed  by  a  stipendiary  chaplain,  and 
that  there  were  besides  within  the  parish  the  chapels  of  Wallsend, 
Heworth,  and  Shields,  the  latter  being  dedicated  to  St.  Hilda,  and 
sometimes,  as  it  seems,  called  the  chapel  of  Westoe  from  a  chantry 
within  it  of  that  name,  all  of  them  served  by  curates,  partly  stipen- 
diary and  partly  endowed.  The  Westoe  chantry  appears  to  have 
been  dedicated  to  St.  Lawrence,  for  the  painting  of  whose  image  there 
is  a  charge  of  2s.  in  1421  (p.  94). 

COLLACIO  CAPELLJE  SANCTJE  HILD^. — Reg.  II.  77. 

Willelmus  Prior  ecclesiae  Dunolra.  dilecto  sibi  in  Christo  domino  Roberto 
de  Dalton,  capellano,  salutem  in  Auctore  salutis.  Reputantes  honestius 
quod  capellse  Sanctae  Hyldae  Virginis,  infra  parochiam  ecclesiae  de  Jarow 
situates  et  dependent!  ad  eadam,  in  qua,  propter  distanciam  locorum,  quidam 
de  parochianis  dictae  ecclesiae  de  Jarow  consueverunt  audire  Divina,  per 
unum  capellanum  perpetuum,  dummodo  fuerit  ydoneus,  deserviatur,  quam 
per  sacerdotes  conductitios  annuatim,  Tibi,  de  cujus  conversacione  lauda- 
bile  testimonium  perhibetur,  ut  in  eadem  capella  tuo  perpetuo  ministrare 
valeas,  percepturo  exinde  loco  salarii  quantum  alii  sacerdotes  in  ipsa  debite 
ministrantes  hactenus  perceperunt,  concedimus  per  presentes,  volentes  ulte- 
rius  ut  domos  juxta  dictam  capellam  ad  inhabitandum  habeas  pro  tempore, 
quo  ibi  te  contigerit  ministrare,  ita  tamen  quod  eas  tuo  tempore  in  bono 
statu  sustineas,  ac  omnia  alia,  quae  tibi  dinoscunturincumbere,  peragas  con- 
gruenter.  In  cujus  rei  testimonium.— Dat.  Dunolm.  primo  die  Octobris 
Anno  Domini  Millesimo  ccc.  vicesimo  primo.

COLLACIO  CANTARI^E  DE  WYVESTOW  CUM  LICENCIA   CAPIENDI  UNUM  AMNUALE. 

In  margine  —  NOTA   PRO   CAPELLA  DE  LE  SCHELES.  —  From  a  copy  in 
C.  iv.  25,  p.  646,  D.  and  C.  Libr. 

R.  Prior  ecclesiae  Dunelmensis  dilecto  sibi  in  Christo  domino  Johanni  de 
H.  capellano  salutem  in  amplexibus  Salvatoris.  Tuae  probitatis  merita  et 
conversacionis  honestas,  super  quibus  nobis  laudabile  testimonium  perhibe- 
tur,  merito  nos  inducunt  ut  personam  tuam  uberiore  munificentia  prosequa- 
mur.  Hinc  est  quod  cantariam  capellae  Sanctae  Hyldae  juxta  W.  vacantem, 
et  ad  nostram  collacionem  spectantem,  tibi  cum  redditibus  ac  universis 
juribus  et  pertinentiis  suis,  quibus  predecessores  tui  capellani  in  eadem 
capella  celebrantes  hactenus  usi  fuerint  et  gavisi,  ad  sustentacionem  capel- 
lani predictae  cantariae,  conferimus,  et  de  eisdem  providemus,  tenore  presen- 
cium  intuitu  caritatis.  Insuper,  propter  dictae  cantariae  exilitatem,  cujus 
redditus  et  proventus,  sicut  ex  testimonio  ndedignorum  excepimus,  non 
sufficiunt  annuatim  ad  unius  capellani  congruam  sustentacionem,  gratiain 
volumus  in  hac  parte  facere  specialem,  tibi  unum  servicium  annuale  perci- 
piendi  per  proximum  biennium  in  sustentacionis  tuae  subsidium  tenore  pre- 
sencium  plenam  concedimus  facultatem.  Proviso  quod  omnia  onera  dictae 
cantariae  incumbencia,  super  quibus  tuam  conscientiam  oneramus,  per  te  vel 
per  alium  debite  sustineas  peragas  et  supportes,  ecclesiam  parochialem  de 
Jarow  sub  debito  juramenti  servare  tenearis  indempnem,  alioquin,  quando- 
cunque  te  premissa  non  adimplesse  contigerit  inveniri,  licebit  nobis  et 
successoribus  nostris  te  a  dicta  cantaria  sine  strepitu  judicial!  libere  amo- 
vere,  et  alium  pro  nostrae  voluntatis  vel  successorum  nostrorum  libito 
subrogare.  In  cujus  rei,  etc. 

LITERA  CITATORIA  MISSA  CAPELLANO  PAROCHIALI  DE  JAROWE  AD 
VISITANDUM.  —  Reg.  II.  H6&. 

Johannes  Prior  ecclesise  Dunolm.  archidiaconus  in  Ecclesiis  Ecclesiae 
Dunelmensi  appropriatis,  capellano  parochiali  de  Jarow—  Quia  nos,  favente 
Altissimo,  ecclesiam  parochialem  de  Jarowe  ac  capellas  ab  eadem  depen- 
dentes,  nee  non  clerum  et  populum  ejusdem  juxta  juris  exigenciam,  pro  ut 
de  jure  nostro  archidiaconali  ad  nos  attinet—  decrevimus  visitare,  vobis— 
mandamus,  quatinus  citetis  peremptorie  omnes  et  singulos  capellanos  et 
ministros  infra  parochiam—  celebrantes—  ac  de  singulis  villis—  octo  sex 
quatuor  vel  tres  viros  fidedignos,  juxta  majoritatem  vel  minontatem 
villarum  predictarum,  per  quos  rei  veritas  super  corngendis  et  retormandis 
excessibus.—  Illi  videlicet  de  Jarowe,  Monketon,  Hebenne,  Folesceby, 
Heword  inferiore,  Heword  superiore,  le  Felling,  et  de  Wylmgton  et 
Walleshend,  die  mart.  prox.  p.  fest  S.  Wilfr.  Episcopi  m  capella  de 
Heword;  nee  non  et  illi  de  Hetheword  Simondset,  Schelles  Wyyestowe  et 
Herton,  die  mere,  extunc  prox.^seq.  in  capella  S.  I

P.  42.  Dommus    Z7fr*fc«.-Thifl  sum  of  20s.,  a  large  one  for  the

Convent  of  Durham,  to  give  a  feast  to  his  friends  upon  taking  ^  his

236  APPENDIX. 

church  of  Durham,  and  her  other  Cells,  contributed  for  this  purpose  in 
the  same  liberal  way,  Ughtred's  incepting  would  be  long  remembered 
in  the  University.  The  inceptor  became  afterwards  an  ecclesiastic  of 
considerable  name  and  fame.  See  "  Priory  of  Finchale,"  pref.  p.  xxvii. 
It  will  be  observed  that  the  houses  of  Jarrow  and  Wearmouth  made 
frequent  payments  towards  the  education  of  students  from  Durham  in 
Durham  College.  See  p.  48  (1365),  in  which  year  the  monks  were 
studying  at  Stamford,  perhaps  on  account  of  the  plague.  That  the  act 
of  incepting,  or  rather  the  feast  which  the  inceptor  was  obliged  from 
custom  to  give  to  his  friends  on  the  occasion,  was  attended  with  serious 
expenses,  is  proved  by  the  following  letter,  addressed  by  the  Prior  of 
Durham  in  1440  to  the  Prior  of  Stamford,  one  of  his  monks,  who 
having  the  cost  of  the  banquet  before  his  eyes  was  reluctant  to  proceed 
in  theology.

Confrater  precarissinie,  salutacione  votiva  premissa.  Laborat  inter  nos 
vox  et  fama,  quod  non  intenditis  in  facultate  theologica  incipere,  in  tantum, 
quod  dixistis,  quod  non  vultis  pro  aliqua  gratia  super  hujusmodi  actu 
inceptionis  impetranda  pecunias  solvere,  aut  alias  laborare,  sicut  faciunt  et 
facient  confratres  vestri.  Super  qua  materia  nos  cum  proximo  inter veniente 
literatorie  certificetis.  Scientes,  quod,  si  continget,  vos  et  confratres  vestros 
ineepturos,  oportebit  vos  et  ipsos  pro  gratia  super  solucione  summae 
solvendae  pro  convivio  non  faciendo  effectualiter  laborare,  et  de  propriis 
pecuniis  notabiliter  pro  hujusmodi  actu  effundere,  sicut  alii  confratres

P.  43.  The  General  Chapter  here  mentioned  was  that  of  the  Bene- 
dictine order,  held  in  that  year  at  Northampton,  the  usual  place  for 
the  meeting.  The  Prior  of  Durham  was  frequently  one  of  the  Pre- 
sidents in  those  Convocations,  and  the  "  Diffinitores,"  p.  49,  were  nine 
persons  elected  at  each  meeting,  "  penes  quos  precipua  est  totius  capi- 
tuli  potestas  sive  ad  electiones  superiorum  sive  ad  statuenda  et  defini- 
enda  quajcunque  monasticam  spectant  disciplinam."  Du  Cange.  It 
would  appear  that  they  were  paid  for  their  trouble. 

P.  47.  Wine  given  to  the  Convent. — The  Priors  and  Masters  of  the 
various  Cells  under  subjection  to  the  Convent  of  Durham  rendered  their 
yearly  Account  Roll  to  the  mother  church  on  the  Monday  next  after 
the  Feast  of  the  Ascension,  and  not  unfrequently  contributed  to  a  feast 
upon  the  occasion.  The  wine  given  to  the  Prior  at  Pittington  (p.  50) 
was  a  like  contribution  to  a  series  of  banquets  given  by  that  dignitary 


JARROW.  237 

at  Pittington.,  one  of  his  manor  houses.  In  p.  48  we  have  a  payment 
to  the  Prior's  singers  at  Beaurepaire,  another  of  his  country  residences. 
See  also  p.  56  (1370),  and  p.  59  (1371).  Richard  de  Eden,  one  of 
the  monks,  seems  to  have  acted  as  Master  of  the  Revels  at  the  latter 
place  in  1370.  In  1364  (p.  48)  the  Prior  of  Durham  was  keeping 
holiday  at  his  manor  of  Westoe,  and  the  monks  of  Jarrow  made  him  a 
present  of  12s.  on  the  occasion. 

P.  50.  The  New  Chamber  in  the  Infirmary.  P.  60.  The  "  Retrodor- 
sale"  of  the  High  Altar,  called  in  p.  169,  &c.  "  sculptura  summi 
altaris.  P.  76,  &c.  The  Dormitory.  P.  89,  &c.  The  Library.— These 
were  all  works  which  were  in  progress  at  Durham  in  the  years  referred 
to,  to  which  the  monks  of  Jarrow  and  Wermouth  were  obliged  to  con- 
tribute. In  addition  to  these  demands  upon  their  slender  income,  we 
find  the  two  houses  making  payments  to  the  mill-dam  at  Pittington, 
p.  73,  and  the  wall  of  Beaurepaire,  p.  43,  &c.  The  following  letter 
proves  that  Lord  Neville  of  Raby  was  not  the  only  contributor  to  the 
altar  screen  at  Durham.

P.  70,  71.  Sir  Thomas  Surtays. — The  manor  of  Felling,  within  the 
parish  of  Jarrow,  belonged  at  that  time  to  the  family  of  Surtees  of 
Dinsdale,  and  there  Sir  Thomas  Surtees,  the  head  of  the  house  in  his 
day,  died  in  1379.  According  to  the  custom  of  the  church,  his  best 
garment  and  his  best  horse  became  upon  his  death  the  property  of  the 
church  as  mortuaries,  and  they  are  both  duly  enumerated  in  this 
inventory.  His  "  dowblet "  the  Master  had  stored  up  in  his  own 
chamber,  and  in  the  stable  was  his  "  palfry."  Frequent  mention  is 
made  in  the  Wermouth  Rolls  of  the  mortuaries  which  accrued  to  the 
church  upon  the  death  of  the  Lords  of  Hilton  Castle,  to  be  hereafter 
noticed. 

P.  80.  Four  vials — and  two  stolen. — On  the  subject  of  this  theft  we 
have  the  following  fulmination  from  the  Prior  of  Durham,  who  was  at 
that  time  acting  as  the  vicar-general  of  the  Bishop.  The  thieves  would 
probably  laugh  at  the  telum  imbelle.  The  monks  of  Wearmouth  sus- 
tained a  like  loss  in  1384,  vide  p.  177. 

LITERA  EXCOMMUNICACIONIS  PRO  MAGISTRO  DE  JAROW. Reg.  II.  ParV.f.  1  b. 

Johannes  Prior  ecclesiae  Cathedralis  Dunelm.  venerabilis  in  Christo  patris 
et  domini  domini  T.  Dei  gracia  Dunelmensis  episcopi  in  remotis  agentis  in 
spiritualibus  vicarius  generalis,  universis  et  singulis  rectoribus  vicariis 
capellanis  parochialibus  infra  diocesim  Dunelm.  constitutis  salutem  in 
Auctore  Salutis.  Gravem  querelam  Magistri  de  Jarow  nuper  recepimus 
continentem  quod  nonnulli  iniquitatis  filii  sui  salutis  immemores — quorum 
nomina  ignorantur  pariter  et  personse  ecclesiam  de  Jarow  fregerunt  intra- 
verunt  bona  diversa  de  dicta  ecclesia  viz.  fiolas  argenteas,  ligaturas  libro- 
rum  et  alia  ornamenta  diripuerunt — necnon  de  ipsius  magistri  celario 
cocliaria  argentea  mappas  manutergia  et  alia — dampnabiliter  rapuerunt — 
quo  circa  vobis — mandamus  quatinus  moneatis — ut  satisfaciant,  restituant — 
sub  pena  excommunicacionis. — Dat.  Dunelm.  in  festo  S.  Barnabse  anno 
Domini  Millesimo  CCCC  septimo.

WERMOUTH. 


P.  141,  The  mortuary  of  Sir  Robert  Hilton,  Knt.  viz.  his  horse  and 
armour.* 

The  Castle  of  Hilton  was  situated  within  the  parish  of  Wermouth,  of 
which  the  Prior  of  Durham  was  rector,  and  numerous  were  the 
quarrels  which  sprung  up  from  time  to  time  between  him  and  its  lords. 
Of  some  of  these  disputes  mention  will  be  made  hereafter.  The  fol- 
lowing interesting  document  proves  that  the  Prior  was  compelled  to 
proceed  against  the  executors  of  the  deceased  Baron  before  he  could 
obtain  the  mortuary  above  mentioned,  and  enumerates  various  other 
claims  which  it  was  thought  advisable  to  include  in  the  "  Bill." 

Infrascripta  petit  procurator  dominorum  Prioris  et  Conventus  Dunol- 
mensis,  ecclesiam  de  Wermuth  monachorum  in  proprios  usus  canonice  opti- 
nencium,  ab  executoribus  doniini  Roberti  de  Hilton  militis  defuncti,  nomine 
procuratorio  pro  eisdem. 

In  primis  petit  meliorem  armaturam  paratam  pro  corpore  dicti  domini 
Roberti,  dum  vixit,  preter  viginti  libras  nomine  mortuarii  pro  eodem,  ex 
consuetudine  laudabili  ecclesiae  de  Wermuth  predictae,  et  dictis  dominis 
suis  ejus  nomine  debitis. 

Item  petit  unum  pullanum  rubeum,  precii  v.  marcarum,  nomine  mor- 
tuarii, pro  Roberto  filio  Roberti  nunc  domini  de  Hilton  defuncti,  ex  con- 
suetudine laudabili  ecclesiae  predictae  et  dominis  suis  predictis  ejus  nomine 
debitis,  qui  pullanus  ad  manus  dicti  Roberti  defuncti  devenit,  et  executores 
dicti  domini  Roberti  nunc  detinent  eundem. 

Item  petit  unum  palefridum,  secundum  melius  animal,  in  bonis  communi- 
bus  dicti  domini  Roberti  et  dominae  Johannae  uxoris  suae,  quondam  tempore 
mortis  Johannse  predictae  existens,  una  cum  appendiciis  seu  pertinenciis 
ejusdem,  preter  viginti  libras  nomine  mortuarii  pro  eadem,  ex  consuetudine 
laudabili  et  prescripta  ecclesiae  de  Wermuth  monachorum,  et  dominis  suis 
predictis  ejus  nomine,  cum  suis  appendiciis  predictis  debitis,  quod  quidem 
animal  cum  suis  appendiciis  predictis  ad  manus  dicti  domini  Roberti,  dum 
vixit,  pervenerunt  (sz'c),  in  quibus  vel  estimacione  quorum,  executores  pre- 
dicti  nomine  executorio  eisdem  dominis  suis  obligantur. 

Item  petit  xx.  marcas,  quas  dictus  dominus  Robertus  dudum  propria 
voluntate  recepit  occupavit  et  asportavit,  de  oblacionibus  et  obvencionibus 
ad  quandam  capellam  de  novo  constructam  infra  limites  et  loca  decimacionis 
ecclesiae  de  Wermuth  predictae,  et  ad  earn  ac  ad  dominos  suos  predictos  per- 
tinentibus  nomine  ejusdem,  in  quibus  oblacionibus  et  obvencionibus  sic 
provenientibus  dictus  dominus  Robertus  coram  suo  Judice  in  hac  parte 
competent!  dictis  dominis  suis  solvendis  extitit  legitime  condempnatus. 

*  Another  Sir  Robert  Hilton  in  the  year  1361  presented  to  the  high  altar  of  the 
church  of  Wermouth  a  cup  of  silver  gilt,  to  serve  as  a  pix  for  the  host.  It  was  stolen 
in  1380.  p.  177. 


240  APPENDIX. 

Item  petit  x.  quarteria  frumenti  et  xx.  qu.  avenae,  in  quibus  dictus  domi- 
nus  Robertus  tenebatur,  dum  vixit,  pro  decimalibus  garbis  sibi  venditis  et 
liberatis,  ex  parte  dominorum  suorum  predictorum,  et  in  quibus  coram 
Judice  suo  conipetenti  in  hac  parte  eisdem  extitit  legitime  condempnatus. 

Item  petit  ixl.  in  quibus  dictus  dominus  Robertus  tenebatur,  dum  vixit, 
dictis  dominis  suis  ex  causa  mutui. 

Item  petit  xl.  in  quibus  dictus  dominus  Robertus,  dum  vixit,  efficaciter 
tenebatur  dictis  dominis  suis,  ex  eo  quod  dictus  dominus  Robertus  singulis 
annis  per  x  annos  dum  vixit,  detinuit  et  detineri  fecit  xxs.  ab  eisdem  domi- 
nis suis,  pro  cariacione  decimarum  suarum  per  loca  ubi  ipse  idem  ix  partes 
cariare  fecit  contra  jura  et  statuta  synodalia  in  hac  parte. — 3.  4.  Spec.  M.  1. 

The  various  other  mortuaries  which  accrued  to  the  Master  of  Wer- 
mouth  and  his  church,  upon  the  death  of  the  other  Barons  of  Hilton, 
in  their  order,  are  duly  enumerated  in  the  Rolls.  In  connection  with 
the  mortuary  banner,  standard,  and  coat-armour  of  Baron  William, 
who  died  in  1505  or  6,  there  is  a  curious  history.  Before  the  year 
1513  they  appear  to  have  been  removed  from  Wermouth  to  ornament 
the  walls  of  the  church  of  Durham,  and  here  they  were  probably 
remaining  when,  on  the  16th  of  July  in  that  year,  they  were  lent  by 
the  Prior,  under  a  bond,  to  the  then  Baron,  another  William,  who  thus 
fought  in  his  father's  coat,  and  under  his  father's  banner,  at  Flodden 
Field,  in  the  October  following  See  the  "  Bond"  in  the  "  Saint 
Cuthbert"  of  the  Editor,  p.  143. 

P.  151.  The  Chaplain  of  Hilton: — Our  first  introduction  to  the  chap- 
lain of  Hilton  Castle  is  in  a  friendly  way.  In  this  year  (1350)  the 
Master  of  Wermouth  makes  to  him  a  present  of  sixpence  for  assisting 
in  hearing  confessions.  Discord,  however,  more  frequently  prevailed 
between  the  two.  So  early  as  the  year  1157  the  Lords  of  Hilton 
obtained  permission  to  have  a  chapefof  their  own,  under  the  walls  of 
their  castle,  within  the  parish  of  Wearmouth.  Certain  conditions,  how- 
ever, were  imposed,  which  are  fully  detailed  by  Mr.  Surtees  in  his 
History.  The  right  of  presentation  to  this  chapel  was  in  the  Baron 
himself,  but  the  admission  of  the  chaplain  was  reserved  to  the  Prior  of 
Durham,  who  bound  the  person  presented,  by  a  solemn  oath,  to  comply 
with  the  original  deed  of  convention.  But  disputes  were  perpetually 
arising  on  the  subject  of  boundaries,  tithes,  and  oblations,  and  the 
chaplains'  oaths  were  "  wafer-cakes."  The  following  documents,  some 
of  them  of  a  very  characteristic  nature,  and  valuable  besides  as  speci- 
mens of  the  language  and  manners  of  their  day,  shew  to  what  an  extent 
those  disputes  were  carried  from  time  to  time.  Nos.  I.  II.  and  III. 
have  reference  to  one  and  the  same  quarrel,  and  prove  that  the  Baron 
of  Hilton  and  his  sons  lorded  it  over  the  poor  Master  of  Wearmouth  in 
a  truly  baronial  way.

We  now  come  to  a  still  more  grievous  quarrel,  prosecuted  at  first  in 
the  Court  of  Durham,  and  afterwards  appealed  to  Rome,  by  Robert 
Staynton,  who  was  chaplain  from  1450  to  1461.  Of  the  following 
documents  the  first  is  a  solemn  excommunication  of  the  chaplain  in 
1459  for  reasons  therein  stated;  the  second  is  an  abstract  of  a  long 
finding  by  a  jury  on  the  17  June,  1461,  not  without  its  interest,  rela- 
tive to  the  chapel  and  its  state  at  that  time;  whilst  all  the  while,  as  is 
proved  by  the  third,  the  chaplain  was  taking  vigorous  steps  in  the 
Court  of  Rome  in  defence  of  his  usurpations,  and  in  defiance  of  his 
oath.  That  he  so  far  succeeded  at  that  tribunal  as  to  obtain  certain 
Bulls  in  his  favour,  against  the  Prior  of  Durham  and  the  Master  of 
Wearmouth,  appears  from  the  most  singular  document  in  the  series,  a 
letter  (No.  IV.)  from  the  Master  to  the  Prior,  gravely  suggest! 
practicability  of  way-laying  and  robbing  (it  amounts  to  thisj  ad, 
who  as  it  was  known  would  on  a  certain  day,  and  by  a  certain  road, 
convey  the  Bulls  in  question  from  Hilton  Castle  to  Wilton  m  Uevcl;  ad, 
where  Staynton  was  then  residing.

CERTIFICATORIUM  dictae  commissionis.     17  June  1461. 

Inquis.  in  eccles.  par.  de  Werm.  Adjourned  to  the  chapel  of  Hilton. 
Present,  the  Master  of  Wermouth,  the  Lady  of  Hilton,  etc. 

The  editor  contents  himself  with  giving  a  brief  abstract  of  the  main  points  in  this 
lengthy  document. 


WERMOUTH.  24.5 

1.  Chapel  in  good  repair.     Repair  belongs  to  the  Lord  of  Hilton. 

2.  Books,  chalices,  vestments  and  ornaments  in  good  condition. 

3.  House  and  buildings  in  good  condition,  save  una  ustrena  newly  built, 
which  wants  a  roof. 

4.  Staynton  a  man  of  good  name  and  fame. 

5.  He  has  not  kept  his  oath  made  upon  his  admission  according  to  the 
'Convention  between  German  the  Prior,  etc. 

7.  Saepius  fuit  discurrens  et  vagans  ad  diversa  loca. 

8.  Absent  from  before  Michalmas  to  Easter,  and  then  staid  only  for  six 
days  together. 

12.  Absent  at  Wilton  in  Yorkshire. 

13.  Duty  done  during  his  absence,  first  from  Mich,  to  St.  Blase  by  a 
Scot  calling  himself  a  priest,  but  having  no  letters  of  orders.     From  St. 
Blaze  to  Easter  the  chaplain  of  William  Bulmer  ( which  chaplain  had  the 
keeping  of  the  tower  of  Hilton)  celebrated.     After  Easter  the  said  Scot 
returned,  and  then  Ralph  Vasy,  chaplain  of  the  Lady  of  Hilton,  officiated. 

14.  Chapel  worth  10  marks  per  ann.  arising  from  land  and  tithes. 

17.  The  lady  seldom  comes  to  the  mother  church,  because  she  seldom 
resides  in  the  parish.     The  other  inhabitants  of  Hilton  do  not  come  when 
they  have  mass  at  home^in  the  chapel — when  no  mass  at  home  then  they 
come. 

18.  The  lamp  is  not  kept  up  as  it  ought,  does  not  burn  all  the  year 
through,  and  yet  he  has  2  cottages  val.  6s.  per  ann.  for  its  maintenance. 

19.  They  never  saw  any  wax  found  in  the  park. 

20.  Has  no  tithes  of  mill  or  fisheries.     Takes  the  oblations  of  Wm.  Bul- 
mer's*  servants  and  ministers  to  them  without  the  master's  leave.     He  red> 
3d.  offered  to  the  crucifix  in  die  parascues  a  year  ago.     He  has  recd>  none  of 
the  townsmen  of  Hilton,  to  the  prejudice  of  the  mother  church.     Since  he 
was  denounced  excommunicate  he  has  not  said  mass  in  the  chapel  except 
once,  die  martis  in  Easter  week.    Robert  Rodes  detains  a  missal  of  the  value 
of  10  marks  given  by  the  Baron  deceased  to  the  chapel  for  ever. 

Monition  to  the  Chaplain  to  reside,

The  following  judicious  letter  from  the  Prior  of  Durham  to  a  subse- 
quent Baron  of  Hilton,  is  worthy  of  being  preserved. 

LITTERA  MISSA  WILLELMO  BARONI  DE  HLLTON. 

Right  honorable  and  full  hertly  welbeloved  Sire,  I  commend  me  to  you. 
Pleas  it  you  to  wete  I  am  informed  that  ye  desire  to  have  ye  good  will  of 
Robert  Shotton,  anenst  a  place  in  Wyvestowe  (Westoe),  ye  whilk  he  hath 
taken  of  me  and  my  brethre,  to  ye  bihove  of  a  servaunt  of  youres  callid 
William  Sluthman,  ye  which  place  one  John  Broun  occupied  whils  he  levid ; 
Sire,  I  will  pray  you  to  be  of  goode  will,  yat  such  Iesse3  as  be  made  be  me 
and  my  brethre,  of  eny  tenandrie  bilongynge  un  to  us,  yat  yei  may  stande 
firme  and  stable,  and  at  ye  will  not  laboure  to  ye  contrary,  as  we  sail  doe 
un  to  you  in  like  case ;  and  at  ye  will  not  take  it  to  eny  displeasure,  if  I  can 
not  fvnd  in  my  conceit  to  lett  eny  tenandrie,  yat  bilonge?  us,  to  ye  said 
William  Sluthman,  for  diverse  consideracions.  And  in  such  thyngs  as  I  may 
doe  to  your  pleasure,  savinge  my  treuth  and  poere  honeste,  ye  shall  fynd  me 
as  wele  and  lovingly  disposed  as  ye  will  desire  me  upon  reason ;  With  ye 

*  This  informant  was  no  other  than  the  priest  resident  within  Hilton  castle  as  the 
private  chaplain  of  Lady  Hilton,  the  widow.  V.  p.  245.

Grace  of  God  who  have  you  ever,  full  hertley  welbeloued   Sire,  in  hys 
bhssid  kepynge.     At  Duresme  ye  ix  day  of  March  (1476-7). 

P.  190.  Pro  unitate  ecdesice.—A  contribution  to  defray  the  expenses 
of  delegates  from  the  Clergy  of  the  Diocese,  in  their  attendance  at  a 
General  Council  at  Pisa,  there  being  at  that  time  two  rival  Popes,  and 
the  church  in  consequence  in  distraction.  The  tax  was  imposed'  in  a 
convocation  of  the  clergy  at  York  in  consequence  of  the  King's  letter 
exhorting  them  to  meet  for  that  purpose,  and  the  sum  levied  was  a 
moiety  of  a  tenth  upon  two-thirds  of  the  real  annual  value  of  such 
spiritual  possessions  or  preferments  as  were  not  taxed  in  the  Nova 
Taxatio  of  1318.  In  other  cases  the  rate  was  levied  upon  the  full 
value. — Taxation  Documents  in  the  Treasury,  Durham. 

P.  194.  Ferilaw. — A  payment  for  crossing  the  river  Wear  in  a  ferry- 
boat. On  this  subject  there  was,  in  the  time  of  Prior  Wessington  in 
the  year  1437,  a  suit  between  the  prior  and  the  bishop's  tenant  of  Sun- 
derland,  into  which  I  enter  no  further  than  to  present  to  the  reader  the 
evidence  on  the  part  of  the  former,  involving,  as  it  does,  so  much  of 
genuine  history  and  so  many  interesting  facts  which  have  been  long 
forgotten.  The  prior  himself  has  the  credit  of  this  compilation. — See 
HIST.  TRES,  App.  cclxx.

(Indorse.) — Alia  evidencia  ad  infrascripta  est  haec.-  Prior  habet,  ex  con- 
cessionibus  et  confirmacionibus  diversorum  Regum  et  episcoporum  Dunel- 
mensium,  omnes  possessiones  ad  ipsum  pertinentes  sub  hiis  terminis  bene  et 
in  pace,  libere  et  quiete  et  integre  et  honorifice,  in  bosco  et  piano,  in  pratis  et 
pascuis,  et  viis  et  semitis,  in  aquis  et  molendinis  et  piscariis  etstagnis  et  passagiis, 
infra  burgum  et  extra,  in  omnibus  rebus  et  locis,  cum  Saca  et  Socce  et  Tol  et 
Them  et  Infangentlieof  et  Wrec,  quieta  et  soluta  de  omnibus  geldis  et  scotis  et 
adjutoriis,  et  ab  auxiliis  Vicecomitum  et  Prepositorum,  et  ab  omnibus  consuetu- 
dinibus  et  operibus  et  auxiliis  et  aliis  querelis,  etc. 

Alia  evidencia  est  haec.  Passagium  batelli  non  est  de  hiis  quae  pertinent  ad 
Regalia,  sive  ad  prerogativam  Regis  vel  aliorum  prerogativas  habentium, 
quia  ut  communiter  passagium  inseritur  in  omnibus  cartis,  etc. 

Alia  evidencia  est  hsec.  Passagium  est  inter  Villam  Regis  de  Berwico  et 
villam  Episcopi  de  Twedmouth,  et  proficuum  inde  proveniens  dividitur  inter 
Regem  et  Episcopum  Dunelmensem.  Item  in  aqua  de  Derwenntt,  currente 
inter  dominium  Episcopi,  de  Houedenchir,  et  dominium  Prioris,  de  Hem- 
myno-burgh,  battellus  passagii  fit  et  reparatur  expensis  utrmsque,  et  nrma 
inde  proveniens  dividitur  inter  Episcopum  et  Priorem.  Item,  rupto  yeteri 
ponte  Dunelmensi  per  inundacionem  aquae,  in  festo  Nativitatis  Beats  Manae, 
anno  Domini  Millesimo  cccc.,  erat  batellus  passagii  et  proficuum  mde  pro- 
veniens divisum  inter  Episcopum  et  Priorem,  et  assignatum  ad  reparacionem 
dicti  pontis,  cuj  us  batelli  custos  erat  quidam  Ricardus  Barbour,  alias  1  sley, 
manens  ad  finem  occidentalem  prefati  pontis.*

250  APPENDIX. 

Ham  Chaumer. — It  is  more  than  probable  that  this  was  the  author  of  the 
"  Continuatio  Historic  Dunelmensis,"  first  printed  by  Wharton  in  his 
"  Anglia  Sacra,"  and  subsequently  by  the  Surtees  Society  in  the  volume 
containing  the  three  Durham  historians,  Coldingham,  Graystanes,  and 
Chambre.  In  the  preface  to  the  latter  edition  the  editor  regretted  his 
inability  to  afford  any  satisfactory  information  with  respect  to  the 
authorship  of  the  Continuation ;  but  here  we  have  a  monk  of  Durham 
of  the  precise  name,  and  living  at  the  precise  period  in  which  the 
writer  must  have  flourished.  Further  inquiry,  therefore,  seems  to  be 
needless.

in  harvesting.

modern  buckram.

horse-shoers  in  paring  the  hoofs  of 
horses. 

BOTTIS,  90.  Vessels,  a  pair  of  which 
are  here  capable  of  holding  six  gallons. 

BOUVESTENGES,  44.     Perhaps  ox-goads. 

BRAUN,  34.  Here  not  the  living  animal, 
but  brawn  in  the  modern  sense  of  the 
word.  They  then,  as  it  appears,  spoke 
of  a  shield  of  brawn.

et  non  spelucata.  If  the  word  farina 
could  be  taken  for  unground  grain, 
then  spelucata  might  perhaps  mean 
deprived  of  its  outer  shell  or  husk, 
like  the  wheat  or  barley  of  which 
frumenty  is  made.  Probably  nothing 
more  is  meant  than  meal  sifted,  and 
meal  unsifted,  the  one  having  the 
bran,  the  other  without  it.
